#### Prompt 4 — Peer Review (independent)

*Reviewer stance: skeptical Stanford robotics professor. Numbers in the briefing were spot-checked against the KH notes (HALOMI 2606.18772, TAVIS 2605.07943, SPIN 2405.07991, DynaMem 2411.04999, Mobi-π 2505.23692). All five headline numbers reproduce exactly, no fabrication. The problems are in the reasoning, not the arithmetic.*

##### 1. Confidence scores (1–10, reliability)

1. **Active gaze coupled to a moving body beats a fixed camera — 9/10.** Verified against four independent KH notes: HALOMI 90%→30% disable-ablation (line 42), SPIN 90–96% vs <26% sim (line 41), TAVIS 47.1 vs 38.8 / +45pp conditional-pick (line 36), ActiveUMI +44%. Convergent, hard, and the briefing is honest that this is the floor, not B2's claim. One point off only because three of the four are sim or own-rig real (the briefing correctly flags this is *not* the novel claim, so it doesn't over-charge).

2. **The novel unit is the decomposition (H3/H6), not the gaze — 7/10.** Internally consistent with the card and well-argued. Docked because "the decomposition is the contribution" is an *aspiration about what would be publishable*, not a verified fact — it rests entirely on the unproven premise that the perception slice is additively separable (H6), which the briefing itself ranks as un-run. The finding asserts the value of the deliverable before the deliverable's central assumption is tested.

3. **B2's whitespace survives a literature check, but barely — 6/10.** The triple-conjunction whitespace (learned look-at × conditioned-on-chosen-base-trajectory × decomposed gaze-vs-exploration) is plausible and the Skeptic's "strip one qualifier and it's pre-empted" caveat is correctly carried. Docked: the literature check is asserted from the analyst's alphaxiv pass, not re-grounded here, and the "conditioned on *B1's chosen* base trajectory" hinge is an *architectural* distinction (Skeptic's words: "not a phenomenon"). Whitespace that exists only at the level of a wiring diagram is thin novelty.

4. **The coupling may be inert (H2 risk) — 7/10.** This is the most intellectually honest finding and the load-bearing one. Well-supported: HALOMI couples gaze to the whole-body controller and works but *never isolated the coupling* (KH line 42 confirms only a full disable-ablation, not a decoupled-gaze ablation), so the field genuinely has not shown non-inertness. Mobi-π's "surpasses human-driven initialization" (KH line 38) is real evidence the base is *a* lever. Docked because the finding slightly conflates "base is a lever" with "gaze is redundant", which does not follow.

5. **Mobi-π is the strongest competing framing — 5/10.** The KH note confirms the factual core (3DGS + BayesOpt single-shot base-pose selection, no gaze, beats human init). But the briefing's *rebuttal* — "Mobi-π is open-loop within a manipulation episode, no in-task re-observation, so it does not preempt the dynamic slice" — is itself unverified and is exactly the un-run H3 regime. The briefing defeats its strongest competitor by appeal to an experiment nobody has run. Lowest score: the escape hatch and the open hypothesis are the same object.

**Mean = 6.8 / 10. Lowest = 5.**

##### 2. Weakest link

**Finding 5 (Mobi-π rebuttal), and the connection-3 "escape hatch" that depends on it.** The entire argument that B2 survives Mobi-π reduces to one claim: *there exists a non-empty regime — an object moving within the current frustum — where gaze is strictly cheaper than re-driving, and that regime is where B2's gain lives.* This is asserted three times (briefing §3, finding 5, H3) and measured zero times. The card's own H3 falsifier concedes gaze may add nothing over exploration. **To verify:** run H3 as written — DynaMem exploration-alone vs exploration+gaze on *in-view vs out-of-view* relocations — and additionally measure what fraction of a realistic mobile-manip task distribution actually contains in-view dynamic relocations. If that fraction is small, the escape hatch is real but commercially empty, and Mobi-π eats the direction on the regime that matters. Until that frequency is known, finding 5's rebuttal is a hope, not a result.

##### 3. Bias check

**Academic over-dominated.** This is not a subtle inference: the analyst's own Contradiction Map (line 40) explicitly names Academic "strongest" and Historian "weakest", and the Prompt-3 briefing inherits that ranking wholesale — the deliverable it recommends (the TAVIS+HomeRobot GT/active/passive triad as "the durable artifact") *is* the Academic's prescription verbatim. The Practitioner is admitted only as a baseline arm to be beaten, the Economist's "this attribution paper has low citation-velocity" warning is registered then waved off, and the Historian's framing is used for one rhetorical flourish ("survives the next end-to-end wave") while its actual caution (modular bets lose on peak SR) is dropped. The briefing reads as an Academic case with three supporting witnesses and one hostile-but-contained witness (Practitioner/Mobi-π), rather than a true five-way synthesis.

##### 4. Missing perspective (a 6th angle that changes the conclusion)

**The sim-to-real / hardware-roboticist angle — and damningly, the analyst itself surfaced it then buried it.** Prompt 2's "blind spot" (analyst line 47) names it precisely: *no card hypothesis H1–H6 tests whether a learned look-at policy survives real head-eye latency, motion blur from a moving base, and rolling shutter* — the failure mode that historically kills active-vision systems. Every cited active-gaze win is either sim (SPIN 90–96%, TAVIS IsaacLab) or real-with-its-own-rig (HALOMI, HoMMI). This perspective changes the conclusion because it attacks the de-risking gate directly: the briefing's COMMIT/DROP decision runs the H2 contrast *in whatever environment is cheapest*, but if base-coupled gaze's advantage is a sim artifact that motion blur erases on hardware, then a clean H2 result in sim greenlights a direction that dies on a real moving base. The de-risking gate must specify it runs on real hardware (or with a calibrated latency/blur model), or it can produce a confident false-positive COMMIT. The briefing's actionable insight is silent on this, despite the analyst having flagged it 15 lines earlier.

##### 5. Overall grade and top fixes

**Grade: B / B+.** This is a careful, numerically honest briefing that correctly identifies the load-bearing contradiction (H2 inertness) and refuses to rubber-stamp the direction — the DE-RISK-then-COMMIT posture is exactly right, and the headline numbers all check out. It is held back from the A range by three things: it defeats its strongest competitor with an unrun experiment, it inherits a single-perspective ranking instead of synthesizing, and it dropped a self-identified fatal blind spot between Prompt 2 and Prompt 3.

**Top fixes, in priority order:**
1. **Quantify the H3 regime before treating it as the escape hatch.** Add "what fraction of the task distribution is in-view dynamic relocation?" as the *first* measurement, not a downstream test. If that fraction is small, the honest conclusion is DROP-toward-benchmark, not DE-RISK-then-COMMIT.
2. **Put sim-to-real of the gaze policy into the de-risking gate.** Specify the H2 contrast runs on real hardware or with a latency/motion-blur model; otherwise the COMMIT decision is vulnerable to a sim-only false positive. This restores the blind spot the analyst correctly raised and then lost.
3. **Separate "the base is a lever" from "gaze is redundant" in finding 4 / connection 3.** Mobi-π proving the base moves SR does *not* imply gaze adds nothing; the two-from-two-directions framing is rhetorically clean but logically over-strong. State the weaker, correct version: both assert the base is *a* sufficient lever *in their tested regimes* (static parking, full-disable), neither tested the closed-loop in-view regime.
4. **Stop double-counting H6 as both "the contribution" and "un-run."** If the separability is unverified, the briefing cannot rank "the decomposition is the novel unit" as *high* reliability — the value of the deliverable is contingent on the assumption the briefing itself flags as the risk.

### B3 (Direction review)

Topic: **the bet** — an *in-policy* memory fusing persistent scene + episodic task-history *and* dynamic relocated-object purging beats *both* static-scene-memory policies (3D Latent Mapping 0.31, mindmap 76%) *and* modular dynamic maps (DynaMem 70%, DovSG 35%) on *multi-room relocated-object* tasks, with the margin scaling monotonically with the number of out-of-view returns (near-zero single-room, maximal multi-room) and dynamic purging specifically required where the object moves between visits.

Reviewer note on novelty: alphaxiv `discover_papers` returned results (worked). The single most threatening real paper is **SOMA (2605.22283)** — an in-policy VLA with persistent spatial-semantic memory *and* a Dynamic Memory Refinement module (relocated-object update via temporal EMA + instance re-association), injected into DiT action blocks. SOMA collapses the "scene + dynamic + in-policy" sub-claim into one already-built system; the surviving whitespace is the *episodic-task-history fusion* leg and the *multi-room scaling-law* leg. Also threatening: **ESCAPE (2604.13633)** "Episodic Spatial Memory + Adaptive Execution" (but ALFRED-sim, modular BFS planner, semantic-grid map, no relocated-object purge, no scaling law) and **2606.00576** Dynamic Resilient Spatio-Semantic Memory (modular, DynaMem-lane).

#### Prompt 1 — Five Perspectives

**1. PRACTITIONER (builds/deploys mobile manipulators).**
- *Core position*: The headline numbers are sim or 6-task real with tiny n; what actually breaks a multi-room memory robot is not the architecture but localization drift and write-quality, neither of which the bet's H1–H4 isolate. A 4-leg in-policy memory (scene + episodic + dynamic-purge + co-trained) is a data and latency monster before it ever sees a second room.
- *Strongest evidence*: EchoVLA's flagship long-horizon real number is **0.10** (TidyBot++) and its real average **0.44** over only **6 tasks**; DynaMem's relocated win (53.3%→6.7% locate-failure) is on a *modular* Stretch system, not an in-policy learned one. SOMA's own failure analysis: **48%** of real OOV failures are *grasp-pose estimation under active search*, **44%** of sim failures are *noisy spatial tokens from imperfect 3D estimation* — i.e. the memory mechanism is rarely the bottleneck once you deploy. HomeRobot's drop from 5–15% to **0.4–0.6%** with real perception is the standing proof that bad detection writes bad memory.
- *Only-this-perspective*: Build the multi-room *evaluation harness* and the *localization-drift budget* first. Across-room SE(3) consistency (re-localization on re-entry) will dominate your SR before any scene-vs-episodic ablation matters; if the map de-registers between rooms, every memory variant fails identically and your "scaling law" is a localization-drift curve in disguise.

**2. ACADEMIC (knows the peer-reviewed literature).**
- *Core position*: The literature already separates the four memory taxonomies the card leans on (RoboMME's temporal/spatial/object/procedural; RMBench's M(1)/M(n) graded amount), and the "memory beats memoryless" claim is settled — the card correctly concedes this. But the card's specific novelty framing ("no one combines scene + episodic in one learned policy, dynamic, multi-room") is *weaker than stated*: SOMA already fuses scene + dynamic in-policy, and ESCAPE already names "episodic spatial memory."
- *Strongest evidence*: MemoryVLA (71.9% SimplerEnv-Bridge, +26pp real long-horizon, +3.6% latency) is the canonical in-policy episodic-only SOTA; SERF (58.7% BEHAVIOR-1K, +23% relocated-goal, but map *not* co-trained, single-room) is the in-policy scene-dynamic precedent with an explicit within-paper dynamic-vs-static control. RoboMME's 14-variant π0.5 pool and best memory-required floor **44.51%** vs **84.08%** symbolic-Oracle vs **90.5%** human is the standardized comparator. The honest reading: every *individual* leg of B3 is published; the bet rests entirely on the *conjunction* + the *scaling law*.
- *Only-this-perspective*: Re-anchor the contribution from "the combination" to "the **scaling-law characterization**" — the H3 monotonicity-with-out-of-view-returns curve is the one artifact no cited or discovered paper produces, and it is benchmark-able (RMBench's TMC binning gives you the x-axis). Lead with the law, not the architecture.

**3. SKEPTIC (thinks it's wrong or already solved).**
- *Core position*: Two of the three legs are already built — SOMA = in-policy + scene + dynamic-update; the remaining "episodic-task-history fusion + multi-room" is incremental, and EchoVLA (the anchor) *already* fuses scene + episodic in-policy, so the card's own anchor partially pre-empts the card's own novelty claim. The "dynamic purging specifically required" sub-claim is at risk because SOMA shows EMA refinement (not ray-cast purge) already handles relocation in-policy.
- *Evidence the card under-weights*: SOMA's Dynamic Memory Refinement (adaptive similarity-aware EMA fusion, instance re-association, "Update" ablation = largest 7.8% drop) is *exactly* the in-policy relocated-object handling H2 claims is unbuilt; and SOMA injects memory into the action DiT (co-trained), unlike SERF whose map is fixed. The card cites SERF as "not co-trained" but does not cite SOMA at all — the strongest counter-paper is missing from the table.
- *Only-this-perspective*: The falsifier the card itself wrote ("if a static-scene-memory policy holds on relocated objects … the combination is not the lever") may already be half-failed: SOMA is dynamic-in-policy and holds on relocation *without* a separate episodic channel or ray-cast purge. Run SOMA-as-baseline before committing; if SOMA matches your dual-memory variant on relocated single-scene objects, the only defensible wedge left is multi-room scale.

**4. ECONOMIST (money/incentives).**
- *Core position*: Memory-VLA is a crowded, well-funded land-grab (Physical Intelligence, Figure AI, Stanford, Berkeley, Huawei Noah, NVIDIA all shipping memory-VLA in 2026), which means benchmark-saturation and naming-collision risk are high and the marginal citation from "yet another memory-VLA" is low — but a *standardized multi-room memory-scaling benchmark* is a durable, defensible asset because it sits upstream of everyone's method.
- *Strongest evidence*: discover_papers surfaced a dense 2026 cluster — MEM (Berkeley/Stanford/MIT/PI), RoboMME (Stanford/Michigan/Figure AI), RMBench, RoboMemArena, MobileManiBench, µVLA, Gated Memory Policy, ESCAPE, SOMA — all within ~4 months. EchoVLA itself is Huawei Noah (industry). The benchmark papers (RoboMME, RMBench, LIBERO-Mem, RMBench's TMC) are the ones accruing comparator-pool citations; the method papers cannibalize each other.
- *Only-this-perspective*: The bet's *method* is racing a field with industry compute you can't match; the bet's *benchmark/scaling-law* is the one piece nobody is incentivized to build (it makes their single-scene numbers look small). Commit to the law as the publishable unit, ride EchoVLA's MoMani / RoboMME / RMBench infrastructure, and let the method be a sufficient existence-proof rather than a SOTA chase.

**5. HISTORIAN (seen prior waves).**
- *Core position*: This is the modular-map-vs-end-to-end-policy swing replaying on memory: the field moved external SLAM/scene-graph maps (DynaMem, DovSG, BUMBLE) → in-policy learned latent maps (3D Latent Mapping, SERF, SOMA), exactly as perception moved from hand-built occupancy grids to learned features. The "dynamic purge" debate echoes the classic stale-map problem in lifelong SLAM, solved there by recency/observation-count decay long before robot learning rediscovered it.
- *Strongest evidence*: The lineage in the card is explicit — modular (DynaMem ray-cast purge, DovSG 13× less memory) → in-policy-static (3D Latent Mapping, EchoVLA static scene map) → in-policy-dynamic (SERF SE(3) tracking, SOMA EMA refinement). Each wave beat the prior on the *same* benchmarks while the harder generalization axis (here: multi-room scale) stayed unmeasured, just as pixel-vs-latent world-model cycles each declared victory on single-scene before scale exposed the gap.
- *Only-this-perspective*: Every prior map-vs-policy swing was ultimately settled not by "which representation" but by *what scale of environment exposed the difference*. The card's instinct to make multi-room extent the axis is historically the *correct* lever — but history also warns that the winning answer is usually a hybrid (learned recall + a thin modular consistency check), not a pure in-policy memory. Budget for the hybrid as the likely result, not the failure case.

#### Prompt 2 — Contradiction Map

1. **Direct clashes.**
   - *Skeptic vs Academic/Card on novelty*: Skeptic says SOMA (in-policy + scene + dynamic) + EchoVLA (scene + episodic in-policy) already cover the conjunction minus multi-room; Academic agrees the legs are published but says the *scaling-law characterization* is genuinely un-built. Specific claim in tension: "dynamic purging is specifically required and unbuilt in-policy" (H2) vs SOMA's co-trained Dynamic Memory Refinement (7.8%-drop ablation).
   - *Practitioner vs Academic/Skeptic on what to measure*: Practitioner says the multi-room margin will be dominated by localization drift and write-quality (SOMA 44% noisy-token failures, HomeRobot perception collapse), so the "scaling law" risks being a drift curve; Academic/Skeptic treat the scaling law as a clean memory-mechanism signal.
   - *Economist vs Skeptic on whether to ship the method*: Economist says drop the SOTA-method chase (crowded, industry-compute-bound) and ship the benchmark; Skeptic says you must *first* run SOMA-as-baseline or the whole bet may be falsified before a benchmark is worth building.

2. **Strongest vs weakest perspective.** Strongest: **Academic** — it is the only one that both concedes the settled parts and isolates the one genuinely un-pre-empted artifact (the H3 scaling law), grounded in real comparators (RoboMME 44.51/84.08/90.5, RMBench TMC, MemoryVLA, SERF). Weakest: **Historian** — directionally right and useful for framing the hybrid-likely outcome, but supplies no falsifiable next step and the lifelong-SLAM analogy, while apt, does not bind to a specific experiment.

3. **The one resolving question.** *Does SOMA's in-policy dynamic spatial memory (no separate episodic-task channel, EMA refinement instead of ray-cast purge) hold on **between-visit** relocated objects, and does its advantage stay flat as task extent grows from single-room to multi-room?* If yes → the bet is pre-empted down to "add an episodic channel"; if its relocated-object SR or multi-room margin collapses → the dual-memory + cross-visit-purge + scaling-law wedge is real. This single experiment resolves the Skeptic-vs-Academic novelty clash.

4. **Universal agreement (likely true).** Every perspective agrees the **multi-room scaling law** (H3) — the monotonic margin vs number of out-of-view returns — is the one artifact no existing paper (cited or discovered) produces, and is therefore the defensible core of B3, whether framed as benchmark (Economist/Academic), correct historical axis (Historian), the only un-pre-empted leg (Skeptic), or the thing that survives drift if controlled (Practitioner).

5. **Blind spot (none addressed).** *Memory-write under partial/erroneous detection at multi-room scale compounds differently than at single-room scale*: across many rooms, a small per-write false-positive rate accumulates into a confidently-wrong map, and **no perspective and no H-test models the interaction between write-error rate and room count** — H5 tests active-vs-passive write quality but only at fixed scale, and H6 prunes footprint but assumes correct retention. The scaling law could be dominated by error-accumulation, not memory-mechanism advantage, and nothing in the card separates the two.

#### Prompt 3 — Synthesis Briefing

**(1) CEO summary.** B3 is a well-constructed bet whose *individual legs are now published* (in-policy episodic: MemoryVLA; in-policy scene-dynamic: SERF and especially **SOMA 2605.22283**; modular dynamic-purge: DynaMem; scene+episodic-in-policy: the card's own anchor EchoVLA) — so the "un-built combination" framing is **partially pre-empted**, most sharply by SOMA, which already fuses persistent scene memory + in-policy co-training + dynamic relocated-object refinement and is *absent from the card's table*. What survives, and what every perspective independently converges on, is narrower but real and defensible: the **multi-room scaling law** — the claim that an in-policy dual scene+episodic memory's margin grows monotonically with the number of out-of-view returns, where single-scene memories saturate. No cited or discovered paper sweeps this. The chief threat to even that is not novelty but confounding: at multi-room scale the margin may be a localization-drift / write-error-accumulation curve rather than a memory-mechanism curve, and the card does not yet separate them.

**(2) Five key findings, ranked by reliability.**
1. *The multi-room scaling law is genuinely un-pre-empted and is the bet's true contribution.* (Support: Academic, Economist, Historian, Skeptic-as-residual; Challenge: Practitioner — it may be a drift curve.) Highest reliability — confirmed by both the card's cited set and discover_papers (BUMBLE is building-scale but modular VLM-maintained, not in-policy; SOMA/SERF/3D-Latent-Mapping/mindmap all single-scene).
2. *"Memory beats memoryless" and "dynamic beats static on relocation" are settled and should not be sold as novelty.* (Support: all five; Challenge: none.) High reliability — BiGym/RMBench/ACT-0.0% floors; DynaMem 53.3%→6.7%; SERF +23%; SOMA "Update" 7.8% drop.
3. *The "in-policy + scene + dynamic" sub-claim is pre-empted by SOMA; only the episodic-fusion + cross-visit-purge leg remains contestable.* (Support: Skeptic, Academic; Challenge: Practitioner — argues SOMA's EMA ≠ true cross-visit re-ID, leaving a real gap.) Medium-high — rests on reading SOMA's EMA refinement as covering vs not-covering between-visit relocation; resolvable by experiment.
4. *Write-quality and localization drift, not the memory architecture, will dominate deployed multi-room SR.* (Support: Practitioner, Historian; Challenge: Academic/Skeptic — treat as controllable nuisance.) Medium — strongly evidenced (SOMA 44–48% failures, HomeRobot collapse) but on different platforms than B3 proposes.
5. *The publishable unit should be the benchmark/scaling-law, not a SOTA method.* (Support: Economist, Academic; Challenge: Skeptic — must first run SOMA-baseline or risk falsification.) Medium — incentive-correct, but contingent on finding 1 surviving the SOMA control.

**(3) Hidden cross-perspective connection.** The Practitioner's "it's a localization-drift curve" worry and the Economist's "build the benchmark" recommendation are the *same insight*: the multi-room scaling law is only worth publishing if it is *decomposed* into a memory-mechanism component and a localization/write-error component — and that decomposition is precisely what would make it a durable benchmark (it forces every future method to report both). The blind spot (write-error accumulation scaling with room count) is the missing axis that unifies them: a benchmark that bins by both out-of-view returns *and* per-write detection error is the artifact that is simultaneously novel (Academic), drift-robust (Practitioner), and upstream-defensible (Economist).

**(4) Actionable insight for the researcher: DE-RISK, do not yet COMMIT.** Concretely, before building the 4-leg method: (a) **Run the SOMA control first** — reproduce or fairly cite SOMA (2605.22283) as an in-policy dynamic-scene baseline and add it to the Related table; test it on *between-visit* relocated objects in a *single* scene. If SOMA matches a dual scene+episodic variant there, the dynamic-purge and episodic-fusion legs are weak and you must retreat fully onto multi-room scale. (b) **Build the multi-room scaling-law harness as the primary artifact**, binning tasks by number of out-of-view returns (RMBench TMC x-axis) *and* by injected per-write detection-error rate (the blind-spot axis), reporting the in-policy-memory margin vs single-scene (3D Latent Mapping, mindmap) and modular (DynaMem, DovSG) baselines as two decomposed curves: mechanism-margin and drift/error-margin. The first concrete experiment is the H3 sweep with the SOMA baseline included and a localization-drift control arm, on EchoVLA's MoMani + RoboMME multi-room extension. Commit to the full dual-memory method only if the mechanism-margin curve is non-flat *after* the drift/error component is regressed out.

**(5) Frontier question that would change everything.** *Is multi-room out-of-view recall fundamentally a memory-representation problem or a map-consistency (re-localization + write-confidence) problem?* If the latter, then a thin modular consistency check bolted onto any in-policy episodic memory (the Historian's hybrid) beats a purpose-built 4-leg fused memory, and the entire scene-vs-episodic-vs-dynamic axis the card turns on collapses into "get the cross-room registration and write-confidence right" — reframing B3 from a memory-architecture bet into a lifelong-mapping-meets-VLA bet.

---

#### Prompt 4 — Peer Review (independent)

*Reviewer stance: skeptical Stanford robotics faculty. Read the card under review (Whole-Body.md, B3, lines 639-721) before scoring. Verdict in one line: the synthesis is honest about its central retreat, but it over-credits the multi-room scaling law's defensibility and under-prices a registration/SLAM-shaped objection that, if right, dissolves the whole bet — not just one leg.*

---

##### 1. Confidence scores (1-10, reliability)

**Finding 1 — "The multi-room scaling law is genuinely un-pre-empted and is the bet's true contribution." Score: 6/10.**
The negative-existence claim ("no cited or discovered paper *sweeps* this") is plausible and consistent with the card: BUMBLE is building-scale but VLM-maintained modular (line 677, explicitly "not the mechanism"), and 3D Latent Mapping / mindmap / SERF / EchoVLA are all single- or static-scene. But "un-pre-empted" is a claim about the *absence* of a paper across the entire literature, and the synthesis has already been caught missing SOMA on the fusion leg — which directly weakens confidence that the *sweep* hasn't been done somewhere in lifelong-SLAM or continual-mapping venues the card never searched. A negative claim is only as strong as the search behind it, and the search demonstrably has a hole. Knock further: "monotonic margin growth" is asserted as the contribution but is an *untested empirical conjecture*, not a finding — calling an unrun sweep "the bet's true contribution" conflates a hypothesis with a result.

**Finding 2 — "Memory beats memoryless / dynamic beats static on relocation are settled; do not sell as novelty." Score: 9/10.**
This is the most reliable claim in the briefing and the card backs every number: BiGym/RMBench/ACT-0.0% floors (line 678), DynaMem 53.3%→6.7% (line 668), SERF +23% under relocated goals (line 667), SOMA's update-ablation 7.8% drop (cited in briefing). Five-perspective unanimous support with no challenger is appropriate here because the claim is *descriptive of published baselines*, not predictive. Docked one point only because "must not be sold as novelty" is advice, not a finding — and the card already frames these as "taken" (line 655), so the briefing is partly knocking down a door the card opened itself.

**Finding 3 — "in-policy + scene + dynamic is pre-empted by SOMA; only episodic-fusion + cross-visit-purge survives." Score: 5/10.**
This is the load-bearing new claim and it rests entirely on SOMA 2605.22283, which the reviewer cannot see in the card and which the briefing itself flags as needing a control run (Insight 4a). The Practitioner's counter — "SOMA's EMA != true cross-visit re-ID" — is exactly the kind of mechanism distinction the card draws sharply for SERF (line 667: "within-episode SE(3) transform tracking, NOT ray-cast purge or cross-visit re-ID"). If SOMA's dynamic handling is EMA/within-episode like SERF's, then SOMA does NOT in fact pre-empt the "dynamic between visits" leg, and Finding 3 is overstated. The briefing rates this "medium-high, resolvable by experiment," but its own resolution path (run SOMA) concedes the pre-emption is *unverified*. I rate it lower than the briefing does precisely because the central downgrade hinges on an unread paper's internal mechanism.

**Finding 4 — "Write-quality and localization drift, not architecture, will dominate deployed multi-room SR." Score: 6/10.**
Directionally the strongest *contribution* of this review-of-the-review: it is the right worry and the card under-defends it (B3's risk list at 714-718 names stale-state and perception-write artifacts but never names *localization/registration drift accumulating with room count* as a confound to the scaling law itself). Evidence is real but cross-platform and therefore soft: SOMA 44-48% failures, HomeRobot 5-15%→0.4-0.6% collapse (line 672) — but these are different robots, different maps, different perception stacks, so "drift will dominate" is an extrapolation, not a measurement. The briefing honestly tags it "medium / different platforms." Correctly calibrated; I match it.

**Finding 5 — "Publish the benchmark/scaling-law, not a SOTA method." Score: 5/10.**
This is a strategy preference dressed as a finding. It is defensible (a decomposed benchmark is more durable and harder to scoop) but it is not *verified* by anything — it is the Economist's taste. The Skeptic's own challenge ("run SOMA-baseline first") is the correct rebuttal: you cannot know the benchmark is the right artifact until the SOMA control tells you whether any mechanism margin survives at all. If the margin is flat, the "benchmark" measures a drift curve and is a SLAM paper, not a memory paper. Rated medium because it is contingent on Finding 1 and Finding 3 both holding, and both are shaky.

---

##### 2. Weakest link

**Finding 3 (SOMA pre-empts the in-policy+scene+dynamic leg) is the least-confident load-bearing claim.** The entire downgrade from "un-built 4-leg combination" to "only episodic-fusion + cross-visit-purge survives" rests on SOMA 2605.22283, which (a) is absent from the card's comparison table — so it has not been read at the mechanism granularity the card applies to every other row, and (b) is asserted to "already fuse persistent scene memory + in-policy co-training + dynamic relocated-object refinement" without the reviewer being able to confirm the *between-visit* (not within-episode) nature of that refinement.

**Specific info needed to verify:** read SOMA 2605.22283 and answer three questions at the exact granularity the card uses for SERF (line 667): (1) Is the scene map *co-trained* with the policy, or are latents frozen before execution (SERF's disqualifier)? (2) Is relocated-object handling *cross-visit re-ID* or within-episode EMA/SE(3) transform tracking (the SERF distinction)? (3) Does SOMA evaluate *multi-room / multi-visit*, or single-scene? If SOMA is single-scene with within-episode dynamics, it pre-empts *less* than the briefing claims, Finding 3 should rise to "weak pre-emption," and the original card's "un-built combination" framing is more intact than the briefing concedes. The briefing's own action 4a (run the SOMA control) is the right instinct — but it should be a literature-read *before* any committed retreat, not an experiment after.

---

##### 3. Bias check — dominant perspective

**The Skeptic over-dominates, with the Practitioner second; the Economist is used mainly to launder the Skeptic's conclusion.** Read the support tallies: the synthesis's two highest-impact moves are (i) the SOMA-based downgrade (Skeptic + Academic) and (ii) the "it's a drift curve" confound (Practitioner). The "hidden connection" in section 3 explicitly *fuses* the Practitioner's drift worry and the Economist's build-the-benchmark recommendation into "the same insight" — which is a tell: when two perspectives are declared identical, one of them is not doing independent work. The Economist here is not pricing anything (no cost of building the multi-room harness, no estimate of whether a SLAM-reframed paper is more or less publishable, no opportunity cost vs B1/B2); it is a rhetorical second vote for the Skeptic's "de-risk, don't commit." The Academic and Historian are present but thin — the Historian's only distinct contribution (the "thin consistency-check hybrid") is correctly elevated to the frontier question, which is to the synthesis's credit. Net: the briefing is skeptic-led and would read more honestly if the Economist either did real cost/publishability math or was dropped.

---

##### 4. Missing perspective — the 6th angle that changes conclusions

**The SLAM / lifelong-mapping systems perspective (a mapping-and-state-estimation roboticist), and it would change the conclusion, not just shade it.** Every perspective in the synthesis argues *within* the frame "is this a memory-architecture contribution?" The frontier question (section 5) gestures at the reframe but treats it as a downstream possibility. A mapping researcher would invert the default: multi-room out-of-view recall has been the *core* problem of lifelong/semantic SLAM and dynamic-scene mapping for fifteen years (loop closure, relocalization, map merging, dynamic-object removal, submap consistency, drift correction). From that chair, the briefing's "novel multi-room scaling law" is at risk of being a *known curve in a new wrapper*: the monotonic degradation of a memory's value with trajectory length is, mechanistically, the accumulation of pose-graph drift between loop closures — a quantity the SLAM literature already characterizes and bounds. If that is right, three conclusions flip: (1) Finding 1's "un-pre-empted" is wrong in the worst way — pre-empted by a *neighboring field the card never searched*, not by a robot-learning paper; (2) the publishable unit (Finding 5) is not a memory benchmark but a *VLA-conditioned-on-imperfect-map* benchmark, which the SLAM community would judge on relocalization metrics the card never names; (3) the de-risk experiment (4b) needs a SLAM-quality axis (ATE/relocalization recall), not just an "injected per-write detection-error rate" abstraction. This perspective is also the strongest *support* for the briefing's own best instinct (Finding 4): drift dominance is not a robot-learning hunch, it is the default expectation of anyone who has run a multi-room map.

---

##### 5. Overall grade and top fixes

**Grade: B / B-.**

The briefing earns its passing grade on intellectual honesty: it self-reports its central retreat (SOMA pre-empts a leg the card claims is open), it ranks its own findings by reliability rather than asserting uniform confidence, and it correctly identifies the drift confound the card itself buried. That is good peer-review-grade self-criticism. It does not reach B+/A because three things keep it from being trustworthy as a *decision document*: (1) the load-bearing downgrade rests on an unread paper (SOMA) at a granularity the card applies to everyone else — the synthesis should have read SOMA before treating it as a pre-emptor; (2) it conflates an unrun sweep with "the true contribution," promoting a hypothesis to a result; (3) it stays inside the robot-learning frame and never consults the field (SLAM) that owns the exact phenomenon it calls novel, so its "un-pre-empted" claim is unsafe.

**Top fixes, in priority order:**
1. **Read SOMA 2605.22283 at SERF granularity** (co-trained? cross-visit vs within-episode? multi-room?) and revise Finding 3 accordingly. This is a one-paper literature read, not an experiment, and it gates the entire retreat. Add SOMA to the card's table either way (its absence is a real gap the briefing correctly caught).
2. **Search the lifelong-SLAM / dynamic-mapping literature for the scaling curve** before calling it un-pre-empted. If the drift-vs-trajectory-length curve is already characterized there, reframe B3 as lifelong-mapping-meets-VLA (the frontier question) *now*, not as a contingency.
3. **Make the de-risk decomposition use a real map-quality axis** (relocalization recall / ATE), not an abstract "injected per-write detection-error rate." The drift component the briefing rightly wants to regress out is a SLAM-measurable quantity; measuring it with a synthetic error knob will not convince the field that owns the objection.
4. **Demote "scaling law is the true contribution" to "scaling law is the surviving hypothesis"** until the SOMA control and the mechanism-margin-after-drift-regression are run. The briefing's own Insight 4 already says "de-risk, do not commit" — the findings section should match that humility instead of declaring the contribution settled.

---

### C (Cluster review)

Topic: the coherence + central shared-bottleneck thesis of Cluster C — "Force-Adaptive Coordination Under Load" — which groups C1 (force-adaptive whole-body control under unknown wrench) and C2 (safety-bounded whole-body control under load) under the claim that *"an external hand wrench propagates through the kinematic chain to the support polygon; the legs compensate for what the arms feel, and the task reward omits the force"*, splitting the load problem into "perform under a force it must model" (C1) and "certify it cannot fall when load drifts OOD" (C2).

**Novelty check (alphaxiv `discover_papers`, 2 queries, returned results).** C1's anticipatory-unknown-external-wrench-forward-model-on-a-humanoid bet survives as whitespace; every real system retrieved is reactive estimate-then-compensate (FALCON 2505.06776, SplitAdapter 2606.03297, ADAPT 2606.16542, Thor, HAFO, Load-Aware 2603.14308, **FAME 2603.08961** uncited) or model-based self-induced (ALMA 2201.03871). FAME is the single most threatening real paper — an uncited, humanoid-specific, sensor-free force-adaptive RL that estimates wrist wrench from $-(J^\top)^\dagger(\tau-\tau_g)$ and conditions a latent context — but it is explicitly reactive latent adaptation, not a forward model of the next-action reaction, so it pre-empts nothing; it is a missing H1-baseline to add, not a bet-killer. C2's wrench-aware-certified-barrier-under-load whitespace also survives (SHIELD 2505.11494 / CBF-RL 2510.14959 locomotion-only; ISSf-CBF 2605.25546 kinematics-only — all conceded in-card). Verdict: **still-whitespace** for both bets, with FAME closing the door slightly on C1's crowdedness.

#### Prompt 1 — Five Perspectives

**1. PRACTITIONER (builds/deploys policies daily).**
Core position: The cluster's physics is right and its anchors are real-robot (FALCON 100 N cart on G1/T1, UAN 113 N drag, Thor 70 kg cart, CMP real 86.7% extreme-OOD), but the C1 headline metric — a CoM-excursion advantage in the *first ~100 ms* after a step load — is at the edge of what 50 Hz control loops and noisy proprioceptive wrench estimation can even measure on real hardware. The bet lives or dies on whether a *learned forward model* of the reaction is more accurate than the analytic $J^\top$ propagation everyone already uses reactively, and on real robots the wrench estimate itself ($-(J^\top)^\dagger(\tau-\tau_g)$, the same one FAME/ADAPT use) is the noise floor.
Strongest evidence: ADAPT (2606.16542) and FAME (2603.08961) both get usable disturbance estimates from proprioception alone (60 N torso pull / 4 kg, resp. 20–30 N hand forces) — so the *input* to C1's forward model is achievable, but both authors stop at reactive observation conditioning, suggesting the next step (forward-modeling) was either tried-and-dropped or seen as marginal.
Only-this-perspective: The 100 ms window is a measurement-feasibility risk, not just a science risk — budget a high-rate F/T-instrumented test rig (e.g. WT-UMI's 1.05 N / 68 ms tactile suit, 2606.13232) purely to *ground-truth* the predicted-vs-actual reaction, or the headline number is unfalsifiable on your own hardware.

**2. ACADEMIC (knows the peer-reviewed literature).**
Core position: The cluster carves the joint correctly — force-as-equilibrium (C1) vs force-as-hard-constraint (C2) is a genuine and underexploited distinction — but the literature already shows the C1 "anticipation" delta is narrower than the card implies: ALMA (2201.03871) proved anticipatory feedforward beats reactive (208%) only for *self-induced known* wrench on a *quadruped*, and RAMBO (2504.06662, uncited) is RL-augmented *model-based* WBC that already blends predictive model structure into loco-manipulation. The honest contribution is "unknown-external + learned-forward + humanoid-support-polygon," which is real but is a 3-way scope-narrowing of an established mechanism, not a new mechanism.
Strongest evidence: The card's own H2 (torque-limit-aware curriculum, not raw force, earns FALCON's 0.60→0.37) is the most peer-defensible sub-hypothesis and is arguably the *real* finding hiding inside C1 — it is a clean, cheap ablation that does not depend on the contested 100 ms claim.
Only-this-perspective: Reframe the lead contribution as the curriculum-feasibility-envelope result (H2/H5), which is robust and reviewer-proof, and treat the anticipatory forward model (H1) as the high-variance upside — do not stake the paper solely on H1.

**3. SKEPTIC (thinks the bet is wrong or already solved).**
Core position: C1's central bet may be a distinction without a deployable difference — FALCON's curriculum and SplitAdapter's factorized history-context already absorb the load reaction well enough that the "first 100 ms" advantage could be in the noise, and the card *itself* writes the falsifier ("if anticipation matches reactive at the same step load, force is adequately handled reactively"). The cluster's weakest seam is that C1 and C2 are bolted together by a slogan ("legs compensate for what the arms feel") but share almost no machinery: C1 is a learned predictor, C2 is a barrier/projection, and the only real bridge is H2-of-C2 (add a wrench term to ISSf-CBF), which is one hypothesis, not a cluster.
Strongest evidence: SplitAdapter gets 26/27 vs 16/27 under 6 kg OOD *reactively* from history; CMP gets 86.7% extreme-OOD *without any force term at all* via learned projection; Sim-to-Real Door (2512.01061) hits 83% on contact-rich doors with *RGB only, no force channel*. Three strong real-robot results that need neither anticipation nor a wrench-aware barrier weaken the "you must model the force" premise.
Only-this-perspective: The convenient omission is that nobody has shown the reactive ceiling is actually binding on a *real humanoid task* — run FALCON-vs-anticipatory on one real step-load task *first*; if the gap is <CoM-sensor-noise, the cluster collapses to "add a force term to a CBF" (a workshop paper, not a thesis).

**4. ECONOMIST (follows money/incentives).**
Core position: This cluster rides the humanoid-foundation-model gold rush (G1/T1/H12 are cheap enough that every lab now ships force-loco-manip demos — FALCON, Thor, SplitAdapter, FAME, ADAPT, Load-Aware all 2025–26, mostly Unitree G1), so the *demo* market is saturating fast and the *certified-safety* niche (C2) is where industry money and regulatory pressure are heading but academic supply is thin. The incentive gradient favors C2: a wrench-aware certified barrier is a defensible, citable, deployment-relevant artifact, whereas C1's "I beat the reactive baseline by X in 100 ms" is one more entry in a crowded leaderboard.
Strongest evidence: The C2 table already shows the field *converging* on certified-vs-reward (SHIELD 2505.11494, CBF-RL 2510.14959 99.0% vs 38.7%, End-to-End 2508.07611 53% unsafe-zone reduction, p-SSA 2502.02858) — all real-G1, all 2025–26 — but *every one of them is locomotion/collision, none carries a payload term*, which is exactly the funded gap.
Only-this-perspective: Commit the publication weight to C2's wrench-aware barrier (the scarce, defensible asset) and use C1 as the *policy-under-test* that the barrier wraps — the money and the citations are in the guarantee-under-load, not in another force-adaptive policy.

**5. HISTORIAN (has seen prior waves).**
Core position: This is the legged-manipulation replay of the model-based-vs-learned and feedforward-vs-feedback cycles — ALMA (2022) is the model-based-feedforward ancestor, the 2025–26 humanoid wave swung hard to learned-reactive (RMA-style latent context: FAME, SplitAdapter, ADAPT all literally cite/extend Rapid Motor Adaptation), and C1 proposes the swing back toward *learned*-feedforward, exactly the synthesis phase these cycles always reach. The danger is the historical pattern where the feedforward/anticipatory term wins in sim and on clean quadruped demos (ALMA) but the model-error tax (URDF + actuator) eats the advantage on a high-DoF humanoid — which is precisely why UAN's actuator net (2502.10894) and the H6 "actuator model unlocks the high-load regime" hypothesis matter more than the card foregrounds.
Strongest evidence: ALMA's 208% feedforward win on a quadruped vs the humanoid wave's retreat to reactive latent adaptation (FAME explicitly "rather than explicitly modeling disturbances") is the textbook feedforward-promised/feedback-shipped split.
Only-this-perspective: The lesson from the DR/sim2real and Dreamer pixel-vs-latent cycles is that the synthesis (learned residual over a nominal analytic model — which the card's own Risk note proposes) is the durable position; lead with the *residual-over-analytic* framing, not "learned forward model" framing, or you re-fight the model-error war ALMA already lost on humanoids.

#### Prompt 2 — Contradiction Map

1. **Direct clash.** SKEPTIC vs the cluster's C1 premise: SKEPTIC says reactive handling already suffices (SplitAdapter 26/27 @6 kg, CMP 86.7% with *no* force term, Door 83% *RGB-only*) so the 100 ms anticipatory delta is likely sub-noise; the card asserts a "measurable CoM-excursion advantage in the first ~100 ms where FALCON's reactive compensation lags." ECONOMIST vs ACADEMIC: ECONOMIST says put the paper weight on C2 (scarce, funded); ACADEMIC says the *reviewer-proof* result is C1's H2 curriculum ablation — they disagree on which half is the safe bet. PRACTITIONER vs HISTORIAN partially clash on feasibility: PRACTITIONER says the wrench *estimate* is the noise floor; HISTORIAN says the *model error* (URDF/actuator) is the real tax — different failure modes for the same forward model.
2. **Strongest vs weakest.** Strongest: SKEPTIC — it is the only perspective grounded in three real-robot results (SplitAdapter/CMP/Door) that each succeed *without* the cluster's proposed mechanism, and it correctly identifies the C1↔C2 bolt-on seam. Weakest: ECONOMIST — its "commit to C2" call is directionally useful but rests on incentive-reading, not on evidence that the wrench-aware barrier is *technically* harder/more novel than C1; it could be talking the researcher into the easier-to-fund but scientifically thinner half.
3. **Resolving question.** On one real humanoid step-load task, is the anticipatory forward-model's CoM-excursion (first 100 ms) advantage over FALCON-reactive *larger than the CoM-sensor / wrench-estimate noise floor* at matched force? That single number resolves SKEPTIC-vs-card and decides whether C1 is a thesis or a workshop note.
4. **Universal agreement (likely true).** Every perspective accepts the cluster's first principle: an external hand wrench propagates through $J^\top$ to the support polygon, so force adaptation is a whole-body balance problem the standard task reward omits — and every perspective accepts that the *reactive* lane is genuinely crowded (FALCON/SplitAdapter/FAME/ADAPT/Thor/HAFO/Load-Aware all real-G1, 2025–26). The physics framing and the crowdedness are not in dispute.
5. **Blind spot (none addressed).** None of the five addressed *bimanual / contact-closure* load — the cluster treats the wrench as a single external force at "the hand," but the highest-value real loads (carrying a box with two hands, a closed kinematic loop through a held object) make the two arm wrenches *coupled and internally-constrained*, which changes the support-polygon math and is not in any C1/C2 hypothesis. H2-COMPACT (2505.17627) and the collaborative-carry papers gesture at it but the cluster's formalism is single-wrench.

#### Prompt 3 — Synthesis Briefing

**(1) CEO summary.** Cluster C carves the load problem at a real and underexploited joint — force-as-equilibrium (C1) vs force-as-hard-constraint (C2) — and its physics premise (the hand wrench propagates to the support polygon) is unanimously accepted and well-anchored on real humanoid hardware. But the cluster is two-thirds sound and one-third stapled: C1's specific bet (a *learned anticipatory forward model* of an unknown-external wrench's whole-body reaction, headlined by a first-100 ms CoM-excursion advantage) sits in a fast-saturating reactive-adaptation field (FALCON, SplitAdapter, FAME, ADAPT) whose strongest members already hit the load targets *reactively* or with no force term at all, so the anticipatory delta is real-but-possibly-sub-noise and must be measured on real hardware before commitment; C2's bet (a *wrench-aware certified barrier under load*) is genuinely scarce — the whole certified-safety field has converged on locomotion/collision with no payload term — and is the more defensible, more fundable asset. The cluster coheres as a *pipeline* (C1 is the policy the C2 barrier wraps) far better than as a *shared-mechanism theme*, and should be committed to as such.

**(2) Five key findings, ranked by reliability.**
i. *The reactive force-adaptation lane is crowded and the load targets are largely met reactively* (SplitAdapter 26/27@6 kg, CMP 86.7% extreme-OOD with no force term, Door 83% RGB-only, plus uncited FAME/RAMBO). Support: SKEPTIC, ECONOMIST, ACADEMIC, HISTORIAN. Challenge: none — only the card's framing, which positions these as baselines-to-beat. *Most reliable.*
ii. *C2's wrench-aware certified-barrier-under-load is real whitespace* (SHIELD/CBF-RL/End-to-End/p-SSA all real-G1 but locomotion/collision-only, zero payload term). Support: ECONOMIST, ACADEMIC, alphaxiv search. Challenge: SKEPTIC mildly (it is "add a wrench term to ISSf-CBF," scope-narrow).
iii. *C1's H2 (torque-limit-aware curriculum, not raw force, earns FALCON's 0.60→0.37) is the reviewer-proof result hiding in C1* — cheap, falsifiable, independent of the 100 ms claim. Support: ACADEMIC, PRACTITIONER. Challenge: ECONOMIST (it is incremental, not fundable-scarce).
iv. *C1's anticipatory delta may be sub-measurement-noise on real hardware* and is unfalsifiable without an instrumented ground-truth rig. Support: SKEPTIC, PRACTITIONER. Challenge: HISTORIAN (says it can work, but only as residual-over-analytic), card (asserts it is measurable).
v. *The synthesis position — learned residual over a nominal analytic $J^\top$/actuator model — is the historically durable form of C1*, not "pure learned forward model." Support: HISTORIAN, PRACTITIONER (+ the card's own Risk note). Challenge: none direct; least *independently corroborated*, hence lowest rank.

**(3) Hidden cross-perspective connection.** PRACTITIONER's "the wrench estimate is the noise floor," HISTORIAN's "the model-error tax killed feedforward on humanoids before," and ACADEMIC's "H2 curriculum is the real finding" converge on one thing the card under-weights: **C1's success is gated by estimation/model fidelity, not by the anticipation mechanism itself** — which means UAN's actuator net (H6) and FALCON's feasibility-envelope (H2) are not side-hypotheses but the *load-bearing substrate* the headline forward-model rides on. The anticipatory term is the thin layer on top of a fidelity stack that three independent perspectives flag as the actual bottleneck.

**(4) Actionable insight for the researcher (COMMIT / DE-RISK / DROP).** **DE-RISK, then commit to the C1→C2 pipeline, not to C1's headline alone.** Concretely: (a) *First experiment* — on one real humanoid step-load task (instrumented with a WT-UMI-class F/T channel for ground truth only), run FALCON-reactive vs a learned-residual-over-analytic anticipatory model and measure the first-100 ms CoM-excursion gap *against the CoM-sensor/wrench-estimate noise floor*; this single number (the Prompt-2 resolving question) decides C1's fate. (b) In parallel and at lower risk, *build the C2 wrench-aware barrier* (add the external-payload equilibrium term to ISSf-CBF's safe set, H2-of-C2) — this is the scarce, fundable asset and does not depend on (a) succeeding. (c) Add FAME (2603.08961) and RAMBO (2504.06662) to the C1 Related table as reactive/model-based baselines, and add FAME's sensor-free $-(J^\top)^\dagger(\tau-\tau_g)$ estimate as the H1 reactive-estimate baseline. DROP nothing, but *re-weight*: lead with C2 + the C1 H2 curriculum result (both reviewer-proof), treat the anticipatory forward model as the high-variance upside that (a) either confirms or kills. Also extend at least one hypothesis to the *bimanual coupled-wrench* case (the blind spot).

**(5) Frontier question.** Once an external load can be both *anticipated* (C1) and *certified-against* (C2) on a humanoid, does the certified barrier's payload-equilibrium term make the anticipatory predictor *redundant* (the barrier already encodes the support-polygon reaction the predictor was forecasting) — i.e., does a sufficiently expressive wrench-aware safe set subsume the forward model, collapsing C1 into C2? If yes, the whole cluster is really one direction (certified-equilibrium control), and that is the thesis worth betting on.

#### Prompt 4 — Peer Review (independent)

*Reviewer stance: skeptical Stanford robotics faculty. The briefing is unusually self-aware and the underlying card (Whole-Body.md C1/C2, L722-896) is dense and well-anchored. I am grading the briefing's claims, not the card. Where the briefing rests on a number, I checked it against the card text.*

##### 1. Confidence scores (1-10, reliability)

**Finding i — the reactive force-adaptation lane is crowded and the load targets are largely met reactively. Score: 8/10.**
Directly verifiable from the card's own C1 Related table: SplitAdapter 26/27@6 kg (L750), CMP 86.7% extreme-OOD (L834, though note this is a *C2* survival number, not a C1 force number — the briefing borrows it loosely), Door 83% RGB-only with *no force channel at all* (L756). The "crowded" claim is unarguable: the table already lists ~13 reactive systems on this axis. One deduction: the briefing says these "hit the load targets reactively or with no force term," but "load targets" is doing heavy lifting — SplitAdapter's 6 kg and FALCON's 100 N are *different* targets, not a single met bar, so "largely met" slightly overstates a fragmented set of partial results. The qualitative claim (crowded, reactive-dominant) is rock-solid; the quantitative "targets met" framing is softer than stated.

**Finding ii — C2's wrench-aware certified barrier under load is real whitespace. Score: 8/10.**
The strongest finding, and the card backs it explicitly: SHIELD/CBF-RL/End-to-End/p-SSA are all real-G1 but locomotion/collision, "no payload/wrench term" (L831, L832, L851, L833 for the quadruped Safe-WBLM). The card itself asserts "no one compares certified vs reward-shaping under load" (L818). The SKEPTIC's own challenge is the right one and the briefing reports it honestly: this is "add a wrench term to ISSf-CBF" (H2-of-C2). That is a real but *narrow* contribution — a single-barrier-term extension, not a new safety paradigm. The "more fundable" claim is asserted, not evidenced; fundability is a sociological prediction the briefing has no instrument for. I dock for treating scarcity as a proxy for fundability without argument.

**Finding iii — H2 (torque-limit-aware curriculum) is the reviewer-proof result hiding in C1. Score: 7/10.**
Maps cleanly to the card's C1 H2 (L775-779): a cheap ablation (limit-aware vs limit-blind curriculum at matched force) with a sharp falsifier. I agree it is the most self-contained, lowest-variance experiment in C1. Two reservations. (1) The 0.60→0.37 attribution to the *curriculum specifically* is FALCON's claim re-asserted; the briefing has not shown FALCON isolated the curriculum from its decoupled upper/lower architecture, shared-observation "mutual awareness" (the card's own H5, L790), or reward terms — so "the curriculum earns it" is a hypothesis, not a result, and the briefing occasionally narrates it as established. (2) ECONOMIST's challenge (incremental, not fundable-scarce) is correct and under-weighted: a reviewer-proof ablation of someone else's design is a confirmation study, not a thesis. Reliable as an experiment, oversold as a "finding."

**Finding iv — the anticipatory delta may be sub-measurement-noise on real hardware and is unfalsifiable without an instrumented rig. Score: 8/10.**
This is the briefing's most valuable contribution and it is correctly the thrust of the DE-RISK verdict. It is well-grounded: the card's headline metric is a "first ~100 ms CoM-excursion advantage" (L743) that exists *nowhere in the card as a measured number* — every cited result is either reactive (FALCON 0.37, ADAPT, WT-UMI 1.05 N RMSE @ 68 ms lag) or anticipatory-but-quadruped-self-induced (ALMA 208%). The PRACTITIONER's "wrench estimate is the noise floor" is sharpened by the card itself: WT-UMI's directly-sensed force already carries a 68 ms lag (L757), and a 100 ms window against a sensor with ~68 ms lag plus CoM-estimate noise is a genuinely tight signal-to-noise regime. The HISTORIAN's "can work, but only as residual-over-analytic" is a real constraint, not a refutation, so the finding survives the challenge. Half-point held back because "sub-noise" is itself an untested prediction — the briefing asserts the delta *may* be sub-noise without bounding either the delta or the noise floor, so the claim is correctly directional but not yet quantified.

**Finding v — the durable form of C1 is a learned residual over a nominal analytic J^T/actuator model, not a pure learned forward model. Score: 7/10.**
Corroborated by the card's own Risk note (L802: "Treat the wrench-reaction predictor as a learned residual over a nominal analytic model; lean on UAN's actuator net where the model is uncertain"). So this is not an independent finding — it restates a risk the card already internalized, which is exactly why the briefing ranks it lowest. The HISTORIAN's "model-error tax killed feedforward on humanoids before" is asserted as historical fact with no citation in the briefing or card; it is plausible and is the standard feedforward-vs-feedback lesson, but as stated it is reviewer-memory, not evidence. Correctly the least-corroborated finding; the self-aware low ranking is appropriate and I match it.

##### 2. Weakest link

**Finding v's historical claim** ("the model-error tax killed feedforward on humanoids before") is the least-defensible single assertion — it is the load-bearing justification for collapsing C1's thesis into a residual formulation, yet it rests entirely on uncited reviewer intuition. **To verify:** a concrete citation or two showing a *humanoid whole-body* feedforward/inverse-dynamics controller that underperformed its feedback/residual counterpart specifically because of inertia/actuator model error (not a quadruped, not a fixed-base arm). If that evidence is thin, the residual framing is a sensible engineering default but not a "historically durable form," and the briefing should downgrade it from finding to design preference.

A close second is **finding iv's quantitative core**: the "sub-noise" claim needs the actual numbers — the expected anticipatory CoM-excursion reduction (from a sim sweep) placed against the measured CoM-estimate + wrench-estimate noise floor on the target platform. The briefing's own proposed first experiment (4a) is exactly the right instrument; until it runs, finding iv is a well-argued hypothesis, not a result.

##### 3. Bias check

**The SKEPTIC over-dominated, with the ECONOMIST second.** Every finding is framed as a threat to commitment (sub-noise, crowded, scarce-but-narrow, redundant-with-C2), and the headline verdict (DE-RISK, treat the headline as "high-variance upside") is a risk-minimization posture. This is appropriate for a peer review of an over-reaching card, but it tilts the synthesis: the PRACTITIONER and HISTORIAN are cited mostly *in service of* the skeptical case (they supply the noise-floor and model-tax arguments), and the ACADEMIC appears mainly to redirect credit to the curriculum (deflationary). The one genuinely *generative* move — the frontier question's "does C2 subsume C1" — is itself a collapse-the-cluster argument, i.e. skepticism pointed at the cluster's own existence. No perspective argues the *upside* of the anticipatory bet on its merits; the strongest pro-C1 voice in the room (the card's H1) is treated only as a falsification target. Net: rigorous, but the synthesis would not have surfaced a reason to *commit* to C1 even if one existed.

##### 4. Missing perspective

**The CONTROL-THEORIST / hardware-dynamics perspective is absent, and it would change the conclusion of the frontier question.** The whole briefing reasons at the level of "anticipate vs certify" but never engages the *timescales and bandwidth* that decide whether either works: a CBF/ISSf barrier acts at the QP/control-loop rate (SPARK's 0.011-0.015 s, L850; Poisson-CBF's 20-100 ms PDE solve, L847), while the anticipatory advantage the briefing chases lives in the *first 100 ms* — i.e. inside one-to-five barrier updates. A control theorist would immediately challenge the frontier question's premise: a wrench-aware barrier does **not** subsume the forward model, because the barrier *certifies the current state's safety* whereas the predictor *shapes the command that keeps the next 100 ms feasible* — they live at different points in the sense-plan-act loop (a filter is a projection on the output; a forward model is a feedforward on the input). The briefing's "does C2 collapse C1?" framing conflates a safety *filter* with a *controller*, and a control theorist would likely conclude the cluster is genuinely two directions for a physical-bandwidth reason, not one — inverting the frontier question's tentative "if yes, the whole cluster is one direction." This perspective also reframes finding iv: the binding question is not "is the delta sub-noise" but "is the predictor's phase lead larger than the barrier's actuation lag" — a bandwidth question the briefing never poses.

##### 5. Overall grade and top fixes

**Grade: A-.**
This is a genuinely good briefing: it is honestly self-ranked, it correctly identifies C2 as the defensible asset and C1's 100 ms headline as the soft spot, the DE-RISK verdict is the right call, and the proposed first experiment is the correct single decisive measurement. It loses the half-grade for three things a Stanford committee would flag.

**Top fixes, in priority order:**
1. **Quantify finding iv before calling it the crux.** Put a number on both sides — expected sim anticipatory CoM-excursion reduction vs the measured CoM/wrench noise floor on the target platform. "May be sub-noise" is the entire basis for DE-RISKing C1; it needs to be a bounded prediction, not an adjective. (This is also experiment 4a, so the fix and the recommendation already align.)
2. **Reframe the frontier question through bandwidth/timescale (the missing CONTROL-THEORIST angle).** As written it conflates a safety filter with a controller and tentatively endorses collapsing C1 into C2; a one-line timescale argument (barrier-rate vs 100 ms phase-lead) likely reverses the answer and strengthens the "two genuine directions" claim the rest of the briefing makes.
3. **Stop crediting the curriculum (finding iii) as established and stop treating scarcity as fundability (finding ii).** Both are asserted. The 0.60→0.37 → curriculum attribution is FALCON's claim, not an isolated result (the card's own H5 confounds it); say so. And "scarce therefore fundable" needs an actual argument (a payload-equilibrium safety result is reviewer-legible *and* deployable), not just rarity.
4. **Minor but checkable: verify the FAME/RAMBO add (insight 4c) and the borrowed CMP number.** FAME (2603.08961) and RAMBO (2504.06662) are genuinely absent from the C1 table (confirmed against L749-767), so the add is sound — but the briefing should confirm they are *reactive* baselines before slotting them as such, and finding i should not borrow CMP's 86.7% (a C2 OOD-*survival* number, L834) as evidence that C1's *force targets* are met reactively; those are different axes.
